People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Nice to meet you too, Dr. Smith. Um, well, I came in because for a while now I’ve been convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with. Which, you know, being a personal financial advisor, client confidentiality is huge. I actually got my bachelor's over at OSU and they really drilled that into us. Go Buckeyes, right? My dad went there too, actually. He was an accounting major, which is a lot drier than what I do, but he loved it. 

But anyway, yeah, these people in the local scene... they're sending me little signals. Like through the invitations I get, or the specific topics they choose for panel discussions, even who ends up sitting near me at these networking events. I usually try to sit near the aisle anyway because I have this bad knee from playing tennis back in college—well, intramural tennis, nothing serious, but I tweaked it pretty good on a clay court once and it still clicks when it rains. 

But yeah, the seating arrangements and the signals... I try to rationalize it. Even when I tell myself it could just be a coincidence, I just can’t really let it go. It's just always right there in the back of my mind.

We'd always stop at this one diner... \n\nBut, um, what was I saying? Oh, right, the signals and the tracking. Yeah, it was about two months ago when I first really started noticing it happening.\n\nDoctor: During these past two months, have you experienced any periods where your mood was unusually elevated, or conversely, where you felt significantly depressed or lacking in energy?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. My mood's been pretty steady, honestly. I haven't been depressed or, like, bouncing off the walls with extra energy or anything. I mean, my energy levels are completely fine. \n\nActually, speaking of energy, I did just switch my coffee recently. It's a great neighborhood, by the way, they've really revitalized all those old Italianate buildings down there over the last decade. A buddy of mine from OSU—the one I used to play intramural tennis with, actually—he just bought a condo there. The exposed brick in his place is just incredible. He works in commercial real estate now, which is funny because back in the dorms we used to argue all the time about whether property or index funds were the better long-term play. You can see the barges going up and down, which is actually really peaceful at night. My granddad used to work on the riverboats, actually. He always had these crazy stories about the currents and the fog rolling in. \n\nBut anyway, the walls in my place, you can hear everything. The guy next door to me, I think he's a graphic designer or something, he plays this really obscure jazz music at all hours. I bought a trumpet and everything, thinking it would be a good stress reliever during finals week. My roommate hated it, obviously. He was a pre-med student, always studying organic chemistry. He actually ended up moving out halfway through the semester. \n\nBut, um, yeah... no voices or anything like that. It's a great neighborhood, by the way, they've really revitalized all those old Italianate buildings down there over the last decade. A buddy of mine from OSU—the one I used to play intramural tennis with, actually—he just bought a condo there. The exposed brick in his place is just incredible. He works in commercial real estate now, which is funny because back in the dorms we used to argue all the time about whether property or index funds were the better long-term play. I always tell my clients diversification is key, you know? You can't just put everything in one basket, especially with how the market fluctuates. \n\nBut, um... wait, what was the question? Oh, right, my mood. Yeah, no, no extreme highs or lows or anything like that. Just normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard any voices or sounds when there was no one else around?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I haven't heard any voices or sounds like that. It's just the signals, really. But you know, speaking of sounds, my apartment building has the thinnest walls. I live in this converted loft space over by Mount Adams—great view of the river, by the way. You can see the barges going up and down, which is actually really peaceful at night. My granddad used to work on the riverboats, actually. He always had these crazy stories about the currents and the fog rolling in. \n\nBut anyway, the walls in my place, you can hear everything. The guy next door to me, I think he's a graphic designer or something, he plays this really obscure jazz music at all hours. Not like Miles Davis or John Coltrane, but this really experimental stuff with a lot of brass. I actually tried getting into jazz back when I was at OSU. I bought a trumpet and everything, thinking it would be a good stress reliever during finals week. My roommate hated it, obviously. He was a pre-med student, always studying organic chemistry. He actually ended up moving out halfway through the semester. \n\nBut, um, yeah... no voices or anything like that. Just regular city noises and my neighbor's weird music.\n\nDoctor: Aside from these concerns about the signals and tracking, have you noticed any changes in your ability to take care of yourself, such as difficulty with personal hygiene or keeping up with your daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Um, well, I'm still working. I mean, my personal hygiene is fine. I always make sure I'm put together, you know? Like I said about the car, presentation is everything in finance. I get my suits dry-cleaned at this place over in Hyde Park—they do an amazing job with the pressing. The guy who runs it, Mr. Kim, he's been there for like thirty years. He actually gave me this great tip about getting coffee stains out of silk ties, which is a lifesaver because I spill things all the time. I'm always drinking coffee, like I mentioned. \n\nActually, I was at this coffee shop last week, trying to prep for a client meeting, and they had this really incredible blueberry scone. It reminded me of the ones my mom used to bake when we were kids. She'd always make them on Sunday mornings before church. We went to this big Presbyterian church downtown, the one with the huge stained-glass windows. The choir there was amazing, they always did this huge Christmas cantata...\n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, my responsibilities. Right. Well, I am still working, but I guess my practice has gotten a bit smaller lately. I've been missing some follow-ups, and honestly, I've been avoiding some of the public speaking panels I usually do. It's just... I've been having trouble keeping up the same level of client contact I used to manage. It's just a lot to juggle right now, I guess.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel that your thoughts are ever disorganized, or that you have difficulty following a logical train of thought?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 32 / personal_financial_advisor / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Disorganized? No, I don't think so. I mean, my thoughts are pretty logical. You kind of have to be highly logical in my line of work, you know? I spend half my day building these massive Excel models for client retirement planning. I actually just learned this new macro function from a guy at the office, Dave. He transferred over from the Chicago branch a few months ago. 

Chicago is a great city, honestly. I went there for a conference back in 2018, right in the middle of winter. The wind coming off Lake Michigan is absolutely brutal, it just cuts right through you. I had this heavy wool peacoat I bought just for the trip, but it didn't do a thing. I ended up ducking into this deep-dish pizza place just to get out of the cold, and man, it was the best pizza I've ever had. Way better than New York style, in my opinion, though my brother would definitely argue with me on that. He lives in Brooklyn now, working at some tech startup, and he swears by those giant thin slices you have to fold in half to eat. He actually tried making his own dough from scratch during the pandemic, bought a pizza stone and everything... 

But, um, wait. Did you ask about my brother? Oh, my thoughts. Right, right. No, I don't have any trouble following a train of thought. Everything is pretty clear up there, I haven't noticed any issues with that.

Read my mind? No, no, definitely not. That sounds like something out of a sci-fi movie, you know? I actually love sci-fi, though. I used to go to those midnight premieres all the time back in college. The energy in the theater was always amazing, everyone dressed up and cheering. I remember when the new Star Wars movies started coming out, my friends and I camped outside the theater for like six hours just to get good seats. 

We brought these folding chairs and a cooler full of snacks. My buddy Eric, he actually made this replica lightsaber out of some PVC pipe and LED lights he bought at a hardware store. It was surprisingly bright. He ended up dropping it in the parking lot later that night and it completely shattered, which was kind of hilarious. Eric was always building stuff like that. He actually became an electrical engineer, works for some firm out in Seattle now. It rains so much out there, I don't know how he handles it. I need sunshine, you know? That's why I like my loft, the morning light is just incredible. 

But, uh, no. No one is reading my mind or broadcasting my thoughts. That's just crazy talk. My thoughts are my own.

Which, you know, being a personal financial advisor, client confidentiality is huge. I actually got my bachelor's over at OSU and they really drilled that into us. Go Buckeyes, right? My dad went there too, actually. He was an accounting major, which is a lot drier than what I do, but he loved it. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, these people in the local scene... they're sending me little signals. Like through the invitations I get, or the specific topics they choose for panel discussions, even who ends up sitting near me at these networking events. I usually try to sit near the aisle anyway because I have this bad knee from playing tennis back in college—well, intramural tennis, nothing serious, but I tweaked it pretty good on a clay court once and it still clicks when it rains. \n\nBut yeah, the seating arrangements and the signals... I try to rationalize it. Even when I tell myself it could just be a coincidence, I just can’t really let it go. It's just always right there in the back of my mind.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling that these people are sending you signals and tracking your client meetings?\n\nPatient: Um, I'd say it's been about two months now. Yeah, right around two months. I remember because it was right after I had my car detailed for this big charity gala downtown. I drive a Honda Accord—nothing crazy flashy, you know? In my line of work, you want to look successful but not like you're being irresponsible with your own money, right? I actually read this whole article in Forbes once about how the make of your car directly impacts client trust. \n\nMy dad always drove Buicks, which I think is just a generational thing, really. He absolutely swore by them. He used to say the suspension was like riding on a cloud, and he'd take us on these long road trips to Michigan every summer. We'd always stop at this one diner... \n\nBut, um, what was I saying? Oh, right, the signals and the tracking. Yeah, it was about two months ago when I first really started noticing it happening.\n\nDoctor: During these past two months, have you experienced any periods where your mood was unusually elevated, or conversely, where you felt significantly depressed or lacking in energy?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. My mood's been pretty steady, honestly. I haven't been depressed or, like, bouncing off the walls with extra energy or anything. I mean, my energy levels are completely fine. \n\nActually, speaking of energy, I did just switch my coffee recently. I used to always get this really intense dark roast from a local place down in Over-the-Rhine. It's a great neighborhood, by the way, they've really revitalized all those old Italianate buildings down there over the last decade. A buddy of mine from OSU—the one I used to play intramural tennis with, actually—he just bought a condo there. The exposed brick in his place is just incredible. He works in commercial real estate now, which is funny because back in the dorms we used to argue all the time about whether property or index funds were the better long-term play. I always tell my clients diversification is key, you know? You can't just put everything in one basket, especially with how the market fluctuates. \n\nBut, um... wait, what was the question? Oh, right, my mood. Yeah, no, no extreme highs or lows or anything like that. Just normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard any voices or sounds when there was no one else around?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I haven't heard any voices or sounds like that. It's just the signals, really. You can see the barges going up and down, which is actually really peaceful at night. My granddad used to work on the riverboats, actually. He always had these crazy stories about the currents and the fog rolling in. \n\nBut anyway, the walls in my place, you can hear everything. The guy next door to me, I think he's a graphic designer or something, he plays this really obscure jazz music at all hours. Not like Miles Davis or John Coltrane, but this really experimental stuff with a lot of brass. I actually tried getting into jazz back when I was at OSU. I bought a trumpet and everything, thinking it would be a good stress reliever during finals week. My roommate hated it, obviously. He was a pre-med student, always studying organic chemistry. He actually ended up moving out halfway through the semester. \n\nBut, um, yeah... no voices or anything like that. Just regular city noises and my neighbor's weird music.\n\nDoctor: Aside from these concerns about the signals and tracking, have you noticed any changes in your ability to take care of yourself, such as difficulty with personal hygiene or keeping up with your daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Um, well, I'm still working. I mean, my personal hygiene is fine. I always make sure I'm put together, you know? Like I said about the car, presentation is everything in finance. I get my suits dry-cleaned at this place over in Hyde Park—they do an amazing job with the pressing. The guy who runs it, Mr. Kim, he's been there for like thirty years. He actually gave me this great tip about getting coffee stains out of silk ties, which is a lifesaver because I spill things all the time. I'm always drinking coffee, like I mentioned. \n\nActually, I was at this coffee shop last week, trying to prep for a client meeting, and they had this really incredible blueberry scone. It reminded me of the ones my mom used to bake when we were kids. She'd always make them on Sunday mornings before church. We went to this big Presbyterian church downtown, the one with the huge stained-glass windows. The choir there was amazing, they always did this huge Christmas cantata...\n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, my responsibilities. Right. Well, I am still working, but I guess my practice has gotten a bit smaller lately. I've been missing some follow-ups, and honestly, I've been avoiding some of the public speaking panels I usually do. It's just... I've been having trouble keeping up the same level of client contact I used to manage. It's just a lot to juggle right now, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your thoughts are ever disorganized, or that you have difficulty following a logical train of thought?\n\nPatient: Disorganized? No, I don't think so. I mean, my thoughts are pretty logical. You kind of have to be highly logical in my line of work, you know? I spend half my day building these massive Excel models for client retirement planning. I actually just learned this new macro function from a guy at the office, Dave. He transferred over from the Chicago branch a few months ago. \n\nChicago is a great city, honestly. I went there for a conference back in 2018, right in the middle of winter. The wind coming off Lake Michigan is absolutely brutal, it just cuts right through you. I had this heavy wool peacoat I bought just for the trip, but it didn't do a thing. I ended up ducking into this deep-dish pizza place just to get out of the cold, and man, it was the best pizza I've ever had. Way better than New York style, in my opinion, though my brother would definitely argue with me on that. Special ability? Well, I don't know if it's a *special* ability, but I definitely notice patterns that other people just breeze right past. Like, for a while now, I’ve been pretty convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking exactly which clients I meet with. It's subtle, but they send me these little signals through, like, the specific invitations I get, or the panel topics at networking events, or even exactly who sits near me at these charity dinners. 

Like this one dinner at the Cincinnati Art Museum... they have this amazing new contemporary wing, actually. I went to an opening there a few months back and they were serving these tiny little Maryland crab cakes that were just incredible. I actually tried making crab cakes at home right after that, bought the real lump meat from this old fish market down by the river. The guy who runs it, Sal, he's been there for like thirty years. He was telling me about how the river levels have been changing recently and it's totally messing with the barge traffic. Barges get stuck all the time now. It’s crazy how much logistics goes into just getting fresh seafood to Ohio, you know? You’d think with modern supply chains it would be seamless, but one bottleneck and the whole system backs up. Kind of like I-71 during rush hour. I was stuck in traffic for two hours last Tuesday just listening to this history podcast about the Romans... 

But, um, anyway. The signals. Yeah, even when I try to tell myself that it could all just be a coincidence, I just can’t really let it go. I know what I'm seeing, it just feels too connected to be random.

Yeah, right around two months. I remember because it was right after I had my car detailed for this big charity gala downtown. I drive a Honda Accord—nothing crazy flashy, you know? In my line of work, you want to look successful but not like you're being irresponsible with your own money, right? I actually read this whole article in Forbes once about how the make of your car directly impacts client trust. \n\nMy dad always drove Buicks, which I think is just a generational thing, really. He absolutely swore by them. He used to say the suspension was like riding on a cloud, and he'd take us on these long road trips to Michigan every summer. We'd always stop at this one diner... \n\nBut, um, what was I saying? Oh, right, the signals and the tracking. Yeah, it was about two months ago when I first really started noticing it happening.\n\nDoctor: During these past two months, have you experienced any periods where your mood was unusually elevated, or conversely, where you felt significantly depressed or lacking in energy?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. My mood's been pretty steady, honestly. I haven't been depressed or, like, bouncing off the walls with extra energy or anything. I mean, my energy levels are completely fine. \n\nActually, speaking of energy, I did just switch my coffee recently. I used to always get this really intense dark roast from a local place down in Over-the-Rhine. It's a great neighborhood, by the way, they've really revitalized all those old Italianate buildings down there over the last decade. A buddy of mine from OSU—the one I used to play intramural tennis with, actually—he just bought a condo there. The exposed brick in his place is just incredible. He works in commercial real estate now, which is funny because back in the dorms we used to argue all the time about whether property or index funds were the better long-term play. I always tell my clients diversification is key, you know? You can't just put everything in one basket, especially with how the market fluctuates. \n\nBut, um... wait, what was the question? Oh, right, my mood. Yeah, no, no extreme highs or lows or anything like that. Just normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard any voices or sounds when there was no one else around?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I haven't heard any voices or sounds like that. It's just the signals, really. But you know, speaking of sounds, my apartment building has the thinnest walls. I live in this converted loft space over by Mount Adams—great view of the river, by the way. You can see the barges going up and down, which is actually really peaceful at night. My granddad used to work on the riverboats, actually. He always had these crazy stories about the currents and the fog rolling in. \n\nBut anyway, the walls in my place, you can hear everything. The guy next door to me, I think he's a graphic designer or something, he plays this really obscure jazz music at all hours. Not like Miles Davis or John Coltrane, but this really experimental stuff with a lot of brass. I actually tried getting into jazz back when I was at OSU. I bought a trumpet and everything, thinking it would be a good stress reliever during finals week. My roommate hated it, obviously. He was a pre-med student, always studying organic chemistry. He actually ended up moving out halfway through the semester. \n\nBut, um, yeah... no voices or anything like that. Just regular city noises and my neighbor's weird music.\n\nDoctor: Aside from these concerns about the signals and tracking, have you noticed any changes in your ability to take care of yourself, such as difficulty with personal hygiene or keeping up with your daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Um, well, I'm still working. I mean, my personal hygiene is fine. I always make sure I'm put together, you know? Like I said about the car, presentation is everything in finance. I get my suits dry-cleaned at this place over in Hyde Park—they do an amazing job with the pressing. The guy who runs it, Mr. Kim, he's been there for like thirty years. He actually gave me this great tip about getting coffee stains out of silk ties, which is a lifesaver because I spill things all the time. I'm always drinking coffee, like I mentioned. \n\nActually, I was at this coffee shop last week, trying to prep for a client meeting, and they had this really incredible blueberry scone. It reminded me of the ones my mom used to bake when we were kids. She'd always make them on Sunday mornings before church. We went to this big Presbyterian church downtown, the one with the huge stained-glass windows. The choir there was amazing, they always did this huge Christmas cantata...\n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, my responsibilities. Right. Well, I am still working, but I guess my practice has gotten a bit smaller lately. I've been missing some follow-ups, and honestly, I've been avoiding some of the public speaking panels I usually do. It's just... I've been having trouble keeping up the same level of client contact I used to manage. It's just a lot to juggle right now, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your thoughts are ever disorganized, or that you have difficulty following a logical train of thought?\n\nPatient: Disorganized? No, I don't think so. I mean, my thoughts are pretty logical. You kind of have to be highly logical in my line of work, you know? I spend half my day building these massive Excel models for client retirement planning. I actually just learned this new macro function from a guy at the office, Dave. He transferred over from the Chicago branch a few months ago. \n\nChicago is a great city, honestly. I went there for a conference back in 2018, right in the middle of winter. The wind coming off Lake Michigan is absolutely brutal, it just cuts right through you. I had this heavy wool peacoat I bought just for the trip, but it didn't do a thing. I ended up ducking into this deep-dish pizza place just to get out of the cold, and man, it was the best pizza I've ever had. Way better than New York style, in my opinion, though my brother would definitely argue with me on that. He lives in Brooklyn now, working at some tech startup, and he swears by those giant thin slices you have to fold in half to eat. He actually tried making his own dough from scratch during the pandemic, bought a pizza stone and everything... \n\nBut, um, wait. Did you ask about my brother? Oh, my thoughts. Right, right. No, I don't have any trouble following a train of thought. Everything is pretty clear up there, I haven't noticed any issues with that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that you have any other unusual experiences, such as believing that people can read your mind, or that your thoughts are being broadcast to others?\n\nPatient: Read my mind? No, no, definitely not. That sounds like something out of a sci-fi movie, you know? I actually love sci-fi, though. I used to go to those midnight premieres all the time back in college. The energy in the theater was always amazing, everyone dressed up and cheering. I remember when the new Star Wars movies started coming out, my friends and I camped outside the theater for like six hours just to get good seats. \n\nWe brought these folding chairs and a cooler full of snacks. My buddy Eric, he actually made this replica lightsaber out of some PVC pipe and LED lights he bought at a hardware store. It was surprisingly bright. He ended up dropping it in the parking lot later that night and it completely shattered, which was kind of hilarious. Eric was always building stuff like that. He actually became an electrical engineer, works for some firm out in Seattle now. It rains so much out there, I don't know how he handles it. I need sunshine, you know? That's why I like my loft, the morning light is just incredible. \n\nBut, uh, no. No one is reading my mind or broadcasting my thoughts. That's just crazy talk. My thoughts are my own.\n\nDoctor: I've noticed that while we're talking, you often move from one topic to another that seems unrelated; does it ever feel like your mind jumps between ideas faster than you can keep track of them?\n\nPatient: Jumping between ideas? Um, no, I wouldn't say that. I feel like I'm keeping track of everything perfectly fine. I mean, my brain has to work fast, sure. The market moves fast, so you have to be quick. Like yesterday, the tech sector took this sudden dive right before the closing bell, just completely out of nowhere. \n\nIt actually reminded me of this time I went bungee jumping in New Zealand. Talk about a sudden dive, right? I took a month off after I got my bachelor's to just backpack around the South Island. It was absolutely gorgeous. The water in the lakes there is this crazy, unreal shade of blue, like pure glacial meltwater. I actually bought a sapphire necklace for my ex-girlfriend while I was over there because the stone matched the water perfectly. She loved it, but she ended up dropping it down the drain of our bathroom sink a few years later. We had to call this plumber, Jimmy, who showed up at like two in the morning. He charged an absolute fortune for emergency hours, but he managed to fish it out of the U-bend. He drove this vintage Chevy truck, fully restored, cherry red. I've always wanted a classic car like that, maybe a '67 Mustang. There's a guy in my building who restores old engines, he's always out in the garage on weekends...\n\nBut, um, what were you saying? Oh, right, my mind moving too fast. Yeah, no, I don't feel like it jumps around or anything. I'm just being thorough when I explain things. I just have a lot of experiences to draw from, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have a special ability to sense things that others don't, or that you have a unique way of interpreting coincidences that other people might not understand?\n\nPatient: Special ability? Well, I don't know if it's a *special* ability, but I definitely notice patterns that other people just breeze right past. Like, for a while now, I’ve been pretty convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking exactly which clients I meet with. It's subtle, but they send me these little signals through, like, the specific invitations I get, or the panel topics at networking events, or even exactly who sits near me at these charity dinners. \n\nLike this one dinner at the Cincinnati Art Museum... they have this amazing new contemporary wing, actually. I went to an opening there a few months back and they were serving these tiny little Maryland crab cakes that were just incredible. I actually tried making crab cakes at home right after that, bought the real lump meat from this old fish market down by the river. The guy who runs it, Sal, he's been there for like thirty years. He was telling me about how the river levels have been changing recently and it's totally messing with the barge traffic. Barges get stuck all the time now. It’s crazy how much logistics goes into just getting fresh seafood to Ohio, you know? You’d think with modern supply chains it would be seamless, but one bottleneck and the whole system backs up. Kind of like I-71 during rush hour. I was stuck in traffic for two hours last Tuesday just listening to this history podcast about the Romans... \n\nBut, um, anyway. The signals. Yeah, even when I try to tell myself that it could all just be a coincidence, I just can’t really let it go. I know what I'm seeing, it just feels too connected to be random.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Lost interest? I don't know about losing interest, exactly. I mean, I still get my work done, mostly. Just in pieces, you know? But I guess I don't have that same spark I used to. Like, I'll just leave client follow-ups sitting in my inbox for days. I used to play basketball every Thursday night at this rec center over in Hyde Park. They had this beautiful old hardwood floor that they kept perfectly polished. Like, full velour tracksuits in the middle of summer. I think he got them from this thrift store over in Northside. Northside is great for thrift shopping, actually. I found this amazing mid-century modern lamp there once, real brass, heavy as hell. I had to lug it three blocks to my car in the pouring rain. My umbrella completely inverted on me because the wind was so bad. Cincinnati weather is just completely unpredictable. One minute it's sunny, the next it's a torrential downpour. It makes dressing for the office a total nightmare, honestly. You have to layer, but then the heating in my building is always cranked way too high, so I'm sweating in my suit...

But, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my emotions. Yeah, I don't know. When my friends text me to ask what's going on or why I haven't been around, I just give them these really short answers. It just feels like talking or explaining things takes way more effort than it should right now. I just can't be bothered.

I mean, my energy levels are completely fine. \n\nActually, speaking of energy, I did just switch my coffee recently. I used to always get this really intense dark roast from a local place down in Over-the-Rhine. It's a great neighborhood, by the way, they've really revitalized all those old Italianate buildings down there over the last decade. A buddy of mine from OSU—the one I used to play intramural tennis with, actually—he just bought a condo there. The exposed brick in his place is just incredible. He works in commercial real estate now, which is funny because back in the dorms we used to argue all the time about whether property or index funds were the better long-term play. I always tell my clients diversification is key, you know? You can't just put everything in one basket, especially with how the market fluctuates. \n\nBut, um... wait, what was the question? Oh, right, my mood. Yeah, no, no extreme highs or lows or anything like that. Just normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard any voices or sounds when there was no one else around?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I haven't heard any voices or sounds like that. It's just the signals, really. But you know, speaking of sounds, my apartment building has the thinnest walls. I live in this converted loft space over by Mount Adams—great view of the river, by the way. You can see the barges going up and down, which is actually really peaceful at night. My granddad used to work on the riverboats, actually. He always had these crazy stories about the currents and the fog rolling in. \n\nBut anyway, the walls in my place, you can hear everything. The guy next door to me, I think he's a graphic designer or something, he plays this really obscure jazz music at all hours. Not like Miles Davis or John Coltrane, but this really experimental stuff with a lot of brass. I actually tried getting into jazz back when I was at OSU. I bought a trumpet and everything, thinking it would be a good stress reliever during finals week. My roommate hated it, obviously. He was a pre-med student, always studying organic chemistry. He actually ended up moving out halfway through the semester. \n\nBut, um, yeah... no voices or anything like that. Just regular city noises and my neighbor's weird music.\n\nDoctor: Aside from these concerns about the signals and tracking, have you noticed any changes in your ability to take care of yourself, such as difficulty with personal hygiene or keeping up with your daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Um, well, I'm still working. I mean, my personal hygiene is fine. I always make sure I'm put together, you know? Like I said about the car, presentation is everything in finance. I get my suits dry-cleaned at this place over in Hyde Park—they do an amazing job with the pressing. The guy who runs it, Mr. Kim, he's been there for like thirty years. He actually gave me this great tip about getting coffee stains out of silk ties, which is a lifesaver because I spill things all the time. I'm always drinking coffee, like I mentioned. \n\nActually, I was at this coffee shop last week, trying to prep for a client meeting, and they had this really incredible blueberry scone. It reminded me of the ones my mom used to bake when we were kids. She'd always make them on Sunday mornings before church. We went to this big Presbyterian church downtown, the one with the huge stained-glass windows. The choir there was amazing, they always did this huge Christmas cantata...\n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, my responsibilities. Right. Well, I am still working, but I guess my practice has gotten a bit smaller lately. I've been missing some follow-ups, and honestly, I've been avoiding some of the public speaking panels I usually do. It's just... I've been having trouble keeping up the same level of client contact I used to manage. It's just a lot to juggle right now, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your thoughts are ever disorganized, or that you have difficulty following a logical train of thought?\n\nPatient: Disorganized? No, I don't think so. I mean, my thoughts are pretty logical. You kind of have to be highly logical in my line of work, you know? I spend half my day building these massive Excel models for client retirement planning. I actually just learned this new macro function from a guy at the office, Dave. He transferred over from the Chicago branch a few months ago. \n\nChicago is a great city, honestly. I went there for a conference back in 2018, right in the middle of winter. The wind coming off Lake Michigan is absolutely brutal, it just cuts right through you. I had this heavy wool peacoat I bought just for the trip, but it didn't do a thing. I ended up ducking into this deep-dish pizza place just to get out of the cold, and man, it was the best pizza I've ever had. Way better than New York style, in my opinion, though my brother would definitely argue with me on that. He lives in Brooklyn now, working at some tech startup, and he swears by those giant thin slices you have to fold in half to eat. He actually tried making his own dough from scratch during the pandemic, bought a pizza stone and everything... \n\nBut, um, wait. Did you ask about my brother? Oh, my thoughts. Right, right. No, I don't have any trouble following a train of thought. Everything is pretty clear up there, I haven't noticed any issues with that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that you have any other unusual experiences, such as believing that people can read your mind, or that your thoughts are being broadcast to others?\n\nPatient: Read my mind? No, no, definitely not. That sounds like something out of a sci-fi movie, you know? I actually love sci-fi, though. I used to go to those midnight premieres all the time back in college. The energy in the theater was always amazing, everyone dressed up and cheering. I remember when the new Star Wars movies started coming out, my friends and I camped outside the theater for like six hours just to get good seats. \n\nWe brought these folding chairs and a cooler full of snacks. My buddy Eric, he actually made this replica lightsaber out of some PVC pipe and LED lights he bought at a hardware store. It was surprisingly bright. He ended up dropping it in the parking lot later that night and it completely shattered, which was kind of hilarious. Eric was always building stuff like that. He actually became an electrical engineer, works for some firm out in Seattle now. It rains so much out there, I don't know how he handles it. I need sunshine, you know? That's why I like my loft, the morning light is just incredible. \n\nBut, uh, no. No one is reading my mind or broadcasting my thoughts. That's just crazy talk. My thoughts are my own.\n\nDoctor: I've noticed that while we're talking, you often move from one topic to another that seems unrelated; does it ever feel like your mind jumps between ideas faster than you can keep track of them?\n\nPatient: Jumping between ideas? Um, no, I wouldn't say that. I feel like I'm keeping track of everything perfectly fine. I mean, my brain has to work fast, sure. The market moves fast, so you have to be quick. Like yesterday, the tech sector took this sudden dive right before the closing bell, just completely out of nowhere. \n\nIt actually reminded me of this time I went bungee jumping in New Zealand. Talk about a sudden dive, right? I took a month off after I got my bachelor's to just backpack around the South Island. It was absolutely gorgeous. The water in the lakes there is this crazy, unreal shade of blue, like pure glacial meltwater. I actually bought a sapphire necklace for my ex-girlfriend while I was over there because the stone matched the water perfectly. She loved it, but she ended up dropping it down the drain of our bathroom sink a few years later. We had to call this plumber, Jimmy, who showed up at like two in the morning. He charged an absolute fortune for emergency hours, but he managed to fish it out of the U-bend. He drove this vintage Chevy truck, fully restored, cherry red. I've always wanted a classic car like that, maybe a '67 Mustang. There's a guy in my building who restores old engines, he's always out in the garage on weekends...\n\nBut, um, what were you saying? Oh, right, my mind moving too fast. Yeah, no, I don't feel like it jumps around or anything. I'm just being thorough when I explain things. I just have a lot of experiences to draw from, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have a special ability to sense things that others don't, or that you have a unique way of interpreting coincidences that other people might not understand?\n\nPatient: Special ability? Well, I don't know if it's a *special* ability, but I definitely notice patterns that other people just breeze right past. Like, for a while now, I’ve been pretty convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking exactly which clients I meet with. It's subtle, but they send me these little signals through, like, the specific invitations I get, or the panel topics at networking events, or even exactly who sits near me at these charity dinners. \n\nLike this one dinner at the Cincinnati Art Museum... they have this amazing new contemporary wing, actually. I went to an opening there a few months back and they were serving these tiny little Maryland crab cakes that were just incredible. I actually tried making crab cakes at home right after that, bought the real lump meat from this old fish market down by the river. The guy who runs it, Sal, he's been there for like thirty years. He was telling me about how the river levels have been changing recently and it's totally messing with the barge traffic. Barges get stuck all the time now. It’s crazy how much logistics goes into just getting fresh seafood to Ohio, you know? You’d think with modern supply chains it would be seamless, but one bottleneck and the whole system backs up. Kind of like I-71 during rush hour. I was stuck in traffic for two hours last Tuesday just listening to this history podcast about the Romans... \n\nBut, um, anyway. The signals. Yeah, even when I try to tell myself that it could all just be a coincidence, I just can’t really let it go. I know what I'm seeing, it just feels too connected to be random.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you have lost interest in things you used to enjoy, or do you find it difficult to feel any emotions at all?\n\nPatient: Lost interest? I don't know about losing interest, exactly. I mean, I still get my work done, mostly. Just in pieces, you know? But I guess I don't have that same spark I used to. Like, I'll just leave client follow-ups sitting in my inbox for days. My umbrella completely inverted on me because the wind was so bad. Cincinnati weather is just completely unpredictable. One minute it's sunny, the next it's a torrential downpour. It makes dressing for the office a total nightmare, honestly. You have to layer, but then the heating in my building is always cranked way too high, so I'm sweating in my suit...\n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my emotions. Am I entirely certain? Well, like I was saying before, I do try to tell myself that sometimes. I try to say it could all just be a coincidence. But then I look at the actual details, like the physical invitations they send me. 

They're always printed on this very specific, heavy cardstock with those rough, deckled edges. There's this little boutique letterpress shop down in Over-the-Rhine that makes custom stationery exactly like that. Have you been down to OTR lately? The gentrification there is just wild. I remember when I first started out as an advisor, property values down there were rock bottom, and now you can't even touch a decent loft for under half a million. My buddy Greg bought a place there five years ago and made an absolute killing on it. Greg actually just got a golden retriever puppy, by the way. Cutest thing ever, named him Barnaby. But man, puppies are so much work. He was telling me the dog chewed through his laptop charger right before a huge Zoom presentation. Can you imagine the panic? I'd completely lose my mind. I always keep backup chargers in my briefcase now, just in case. You never know when your battery is gonna die, especially with how much these new financial modeling apps drain the CPU on my laptop. I had to upgrade my RAM twice last year just to keep up...

But, um, what was the question? Oh, if I'm misinterpreting things. Yeah, I mean, even when I sit there and try to convince myself I'm misinterpreting who they seat me next to or what the panel topics are, I just can't really let it go. I'm still convinced those arts and finance people are tracking my clients. The signals are just too specific to ignore.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF20.81, F21\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, I'm Dr. Smith. It's nice to meet you; could you tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Nice to meet you too, Dr. Smith. Um, well, I came in because for a while now I’ve been convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with. Which, you know, being a personal financial advisor, client confidentiality is huge. I actually got my bachelor's over at OSU and they really drilled that into us. Go Buckeyes, right? My dad went there too, actually. He was an accounting major, which is a lot drier than what I do, but he loved it. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, these people in the local scene... they're sending me little signals. Like through the invitations I get, or the specific topics they choose for panel discussions, even who ends up sitting near me at these networking events. I usually try to sit near the aisle anyway because I have this bad knee from playing tennis back in college—well, intramural tennis, nothing serious, but I tweaked it pretty good on a clay court once and it still clicks when it rains. \n\nBut yeah, the seating arrangements and the signals... I try to rationalize it. Even when I tell myself it could just be a coincidence, I just can’t really let it go. It's just always right there in the back of my mind.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling that these people are sending you signals and tracking your client meetings?\n\nPatient: Um, I'd say it's been about two months now. Yeah, right around two months. I remember because it was right after I had my car detailed for this big charity gala downtown. I drive a Honda Accord—nothing crazy flashy, you know? In my line of work, you want to look successful but not like you're being irresponsible with your own money, right? I actually read this whole article in Forbes once about how the make of your car directly impacts client trust. \n\nMy dad always drove Buicks, which I think is just a generational thing, really. He absolutely swore by them. He used to say the suspension was like riding on a cloud, and he'd take us on these long road trips to Michigan every summer. Yeah, it was about two months ago when I first really started noticing it happening.\n\nDoctor: During these past two months, have you experienced any periods where your mood was unusually elevated, or conversely, where you felt significantly depressed or lacking in energy?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. Oh, right, my mood. Yeah, no, no extreme highs or lows or anything like that. Just normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard any voices or sounds when there was no one else around?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I haven't heard any voices or sounds like that. It's just the signals, really. But you know, speaking of sounds, my apartment building has the thinnest walls. I live in this converted loft space over by Mount Adams—great view of the river, by the way. You can see the barges going up and down, which is actually really peaceful at night. My granddad used to work on the riverboats, actually. He always had these crazy stories about the currents and the fog rolling in. \n\nBut anyway, the walls in my place, you can hear everything. The guy next door to me, I think he's a graphic designer or something, he plays this really obscure jazz music at all hours. Not like Miles Davis or John Coltrane, but this really experimental stuff with a lot of brass. I actually tried getting into jazz back when I was at OSU. I bought a trumpet and everything, thinking it would be a good stress reliever during finals week. My roommate hated it, obviously. He was a pre-med student, always studying organic chemistry. He actually ended up moving out halfway through the semester. \n\nBut, um, yeah... no voices or anything like that. Just regular city noises and my neighbor's weird music.\n\nDoctor: Aside from these concerns about the signals and tracking, have you noticed any changes in your ability to take care of yourself, such as difficulty with personal hygiene or keeping up with your daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Um, well, I'm still working. I mean, my personal hygiene is fine. I always make sure I'm put together, you know? Like I said about the car, presentation is everything in finance. I get my suits dry-cleaned at this place over in Hyde Park—they do an amazing job with the pressing. The guy who runs it, Mr. Kim, he's been there for like thirty years. He actually gave me this great tip about getting coffee stains out of silk ties, which is a lifesaver because I spill things all the time. I'm always drinking coffee, like I mentioned. \n\nActually, I was at this coffee shop last week, trying to prep for a client meeting, and they had this really incredible blueberry scone. It reminded me of the ones my mom used to bake when we were kids. She'd always make them on Sunday mornings before church. We went to this big Presbyterian church downtown, the one with the huge stained-glass windows. The choir there was amazing, they always did this huge Christmas cantata...\n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, my responsibilities. Right. Well, I am still working, but I guess my practice has gotten a bit smaller lately. I've been missing some follow-ups, and honestly, I've been avoiding some of the public speaking panels I usually do. It's just... I've been having trouble keeping up the same level of client contact I used to manage. It's just a lot to juggle right now, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your thoughts are ever disorganized, or that you have difficulty following a logical train of thought?\n\nPatient: Disorganized? No, I don't think so. I mean, my thoughts are pretty logical. You kind of have to be highly logical in my line of work, you know? I spend half my day building these massive Excel models for client retirement planning. I actually just learned this new macro function from a guy at the office, Dave. He transferred over from the Chicago branch a few months ago. \n\nChicago is a great city, honestly. I went there for a conference back in 2018, right in the middle of winter. The wind coming off Lake Michigan is absolutely brutal, it just cuts right through you. I had this heavy wool peacoat I bought just for the trip, but it didn't do a thing. I ended up ducking into this deep-dish pizza place just to get out of the cold, and man, it was the best pizza I've ever had. Way better than New York style, in my opinion, though my brother would definitely argue with me on that. He lives in Brooklyn now, working at some tech startup, and he swears by those giant thin slices you have to fold in half to eat. He actually tried making his own dough from scratch during the pandemic, bought a pizza stone and everything... \n\nBut, um, wait. Did you ask about my brother? Oh, my thoughts. Right, right. No, I don't have any trouble following a train of thought. Everything is pretty clear up there, I haven't noticed any issues with that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that you have any other unusual experiences, such as believing that people can read your mind, or that your thoughts are being broadcast to others?\n\nPatient: Read my mind? No, no, definitely not. That sounds like something out of a sci-fi movie, you know? I actually love sci-fi, though. I used to go to those midnight premieres all the time back in college. The energy in the theater was always amazing, everyone dressed up and cheering. I remember when the new Star Wars movies started coming out, my friends and I camped outside the theater for like six hours just to get good seats. \n\nWe brought these folding chairs and a cooler full of snacks. My buddy Eric, he actually made this replica lightsaber out of some PVC pipe and LED lights he bought at a hardware store. It was surprisingly bright. He ended up dropping it in the parking lot later that night and it completely shattered, which was kind of hilarious. Eric was always building stuff like that. He actually became an electrical engineer, works for some firm out in Seattle now. It rains so much out there, I don't know how he handles it. I need sunshine, you know? That's why I like my loft, the morning light is just incredible. \n\nBut, uh, no. No one is reading my mind or broadcasting my thoughts. That's just crazy talk. My thoughts are my own.\n\nDoctor: I've noticed that while we're talking, you often move from one topic to another that seems unrelated; does it ever feel like your mind jumps between ideas faster than you can keep track of them?\n\nPatient: Jumping between ideas? Um, no, I wouldn't say that. I feel like I'm keeping track of everything perfectly fine. I mean, my brain has to work fast, sure. The market moves fast, so you have to be quick. Like yesterday, the tech sector took this sudden dive right before the closing bell, just completely out of nowhere. \n\nIt actually reminded me of this time I went bungee jumping in New Zealand. Talk about a sudden dive, right? I took a month off after I got my bachelor's to just backpack around the South Island. It was absolutely gorgeous. The water in the lakes there is this crazy, unreal shade of blue, like pure glacial meltwater. I actually bought a sapphire necklace for my ex-girlfriend while I was over there because the stone matched the water perfectly. She loved it, but she ended up dropping it down the drain of our bathroom sink a few years later. We had to call this plumber, Jimmy, who showed up at like two in the morning. He charged an absolute fortune for emergency hours, but he managed to fish it out of the U-bend. He drove this vintage Chevy truck, fully restored, cherry red. I've always wanted a classic car like that, maybe a '67 Mustang. There's a guy in my building who restores old engines, he's always out in the garage on weekends...\n\nBut, um, what were you saying? Oh, right, my mind moving too fast. Yeah, no, I don't feel like it jumps around or anything. I'm just being thorough when I explain things. I just have a lot of experiences to draw from, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have a special ability to sense things that others don't, or that you have a unique way of interpreting coincidences that other people might not understand?\n\nPatient: Special ability? Well, I don't know if it's a *special* ability, but I definitely notice patterns that other people just breeze right past. Like, for a while now, I’ve been pretty convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking exactly which clients I meet with. It's subtle, but they send me these little signals through, like, the specific invitations I get, or the panel topics at networking events, or even exactly who sits near me at these charity dinners. \n\nLike this one dinner at the Cincinnati Art Museum... they have this amazing new contemporary wing, actually. I went to an opening there a few months back and they were serving these tiny little Maryland crab cakes that were just incredible. I actually tried making crab cakes at home right after that, bought the real lump meat from this old fish market down by the river. The guy who runs it, Sal, he's been there for like thirty years. He was telling me about how the river levels have been changing recently and it's totally messing with the barge traffic. Barges get stuck all the time now. It’s crazy how much logistics goes into just getting fresh seafood to Ohio, you know? You’d think with modern supply chains it would be seamless, but one bottleneck and the whole system backs up. Kind of like I-71 during rush hour. I was stuck in traffic for two hours last Tuesday just listening to this history podcast about the Romans... \n\nBut, um, anyway. The signals. Yeah, even when I try to tell myself that it could all just be a coincidence, I just can’t really let it go. I know what I'm seeing, it just feels too connected to be random.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you have lost interest in things you used to enjoy, or do you find it difficult to feel any emotions at all?\n\nPatient: Lost interest? I don't know about losing interest, exactly. I mean, I still get my work done, mostly. Just in pieces, you know? But I guess I don't have that same spark I used to. Like, I'll just leave client follow-ups sitting in my inbox for days. And I haven't been going to jazz nights downtown or playing pickup basketball lately, and I don't even really care that I'm missing it. \n\nI used to play basketball every Thursday night at this rec center over in Hyde Park. They had this beautiful old hardwood floor that they kept perfectly polished. You had to wear specific non-marking shoes or the guy who ran the desk, Mr. Henderson, would yell at you. He was this grumpy older guy, always wearing these vintage tracksuits. Like, full velour tracksuits in the middle of summer. I think he got them from this thrift store over in Northside. Northside is great for thrift shopping, actually. I found this amazing mid-century modern lamp there once, real brass, heavy as hell. I had to lug it three blocks to my car in the pouring rain. My umbrella completely inverted on me because the wind was so bad. Cincinnati weather is just completely unpredictable. One minute it's sunny, the next it's a torrential downpour. It makes dressing for the office a total nightmare, honestly. You have to layer, but then the heating in my building is always cranked way too high, so I'm sweating in my suit...\n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my emotions. Yeah, I don't know. When my friends text me to ask what's going on or why I haven't been around, I just give them these really short answers. It just feels like talking or explaining things takes way more effort than it should right now. I just can't be bothered.\n\nDoctor: When you see these signals, do you feel entirely certain that these people are intentionally communicating with you, or is there a part of you that wonders if you might be misinterpreting these events?\n\nPatient: Am I entirely certain? Well, like I was saying before, I do try to tell myself that sometimes. I try to say it could all just be a coincidence. But then I look at the actual details, like the physical invitations they send me. \n\nThey're always printed on this very specific, heavy cardstock with those rough, deckled edges. There's this little boutique letterpress shop down in Over-the-Rhine that makes custom stationery exactly like that. Have you been down to OTR lately? The gentrification there is just wild. I remember when I first started out as an advisor, property values down there were rock bottom, and now you can't even touch a decent loft for under half a million. My buddy Greg bought a place there five years ago and made an absolute killing on it. Greg actually just got a golden retriever puppy, by the way. Cutest thing ever, named him Barnaby. But man, puppies are so much work. He was telling me the dog chewed through his laptop charger right before a huge Zoom presentation. Can you imagine the panic? I'd completely lose my mind. I always keep backup chargers in my briefcase now, just in case. You never know when your battery is gonna die, especially with how much these new financial modeling apps drain the CPU on my laptop. I had to upgrade my RAM twice last year just to keep up...\n\nBut, um, what was the question? Oh, if I'm misinterpreting things. Yeah, I mean, even when I sit there and try to convince myself I'm misinterpreting who they seat me next to or what the panel topics are, I just can't really let it go. I'm still convinced those arts and finance people are tracking my clients. The signals are just too specific to ignore.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

He actually tried making his own dough from scratch during the pandemic, bought a pizza stone and everything... \n\nBut, um, wait. Did you ask about my brother? Oh, my thoughts. Right, right. No, I don't have any trouble following a train of thought. Everything is pretty clear up there, I haven't noticed any issues with that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that you have any other unusual experiences, such as believing that people can read your mind, or that your thoughts are being broadcast to others?\n\nPatient: Read my mind? No, no, definitely not. That sounds like something out of a sci-fi movie, you know? I actually love sci-fi, though. I used to go to those midnight premieres all the time back in college. The energy in the theater was always amazing, everyone dressed up and cheering. I remember when the new Star Wars movies started coming out, my friends and I camped outside the theater for like six hours just to get good seats. \n\nWe brought these folding chairs and a cooler full of snacks. My buddy Eric, he actually made this replica lightsaber out of some PVC pipe and LED lights he bought at a hardware store. It was surprisingly bright. He ended up dropping it in the parking lot later that night and it completely shattered, which was kind of hilarious. Eric was always building stuff like that. He actually became an electrical engineer, works for some firm out in Seattle now. It rains so much out there, I don't know how he handles it. I need sunshine, you know? That's why I like my loft, the morning light is just incredible. \n\nBut, uh, no. No one is reading my mind or broadcasting my thoughts. That's just crazy talk. My thoughts are my own.\n\nDoctor: I've noticed that while we're talking, you often move from one topic to another that seems unrelated; does it ever feel like your mind jumps between ideas faster than you can keep track of them?\n\nPatient: Jumping between ideas? Um, no, I wouldn't say that. I feel like I'm keeping track of everything perfectly fine. I mean, my brain has to work fast, sure. The market moves fast, so you have to be quick. Like yesterday, the tech sector took this sudden dive right before the closing bell, just completely out of nowhere. \n\nIt actually reminded me of this time I went bungee jumping in New Zealand. Talk about a sudden dive, right? I took a month off after I got my bachelor's to just backpack around the South Island. It was absolutely gorgeous. The water in the lakes there is this crazy, unreal shade of blue, like pure glacial meltwater. I actually bought a sapphire necklace for my ex-girlfriend while I was over there because the stone matched the water perfectly. She loved it, but she ended up dropping it down the drain of our bathroom sink a few years later. We had to call this plumber, Jimmy, who showed up at like two in the morning. He charged an absolute fortune for emergency hours, but he managed to fish it out of the U-bend. He drove this vintage Chevy truck, fully restored, cherry red. I've always wanted a classic car like that, maybe a '67 Mustang. There's a guy in my building who restores old engines, he's always out in the garage on weekends...\n\nBut, um, what were you saying? Oh, right, my mind moving too fast. Yeah, no, I don't feel like it jumps around or anything. I'm just being thorough when I explain things. I just have a lot of experiences to draw from, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have a special ability to sense things that others don't, or that you have a unique way of interpreting coincidences that other people might not understand?\n\nPatient: Special ability? Well, I don't know if it's a *special* ability, but I definitely notice patterns that other people just breeze right past. Like, for a while now, I’ve been pretty convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking exactly which clients I meet with. It's subtle, but they send me these little signals through, like, the specific invitations I get, or the panel topics at networking events, or even exactly who sits near me at these charity dinners. \n\nLike this one dinner at the Cincinnati Art Museum... they have this amazing new contemporary wing, actually. I went to an opening there a few months back and they were serving these tiny little Maryland crab cakes that were just incredible. I actually tried making crab cakes at home right after that, bought the real lump meat from this old fish market down by the river. The guy who runs it, Sal, he's been there for like thirty years. He was telling me about how the river levels have been changing recently and it's totally messing with the barge traffic. Barges get stuck all the time now. It’s crazy how much logistics goes into just getting fresh seafood to Ohio, you know? You’d think with modern supply chains it would be seamless, but one bottleneck and the whole system backs up. Kind of like I-71 during rush hour. I was stuck in traffic for two hours last Tuesday just listening to this history podcast about the Romans... \n\nBut, um, anyway. The signals. Yeah, even when I try to tell myself that it could all just be a coincidence, I just can’t really let it go. I know what I'm seeing, it just feels too connected to be random.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you have lost interest in things you used to enjoy, or do you find it difficult to feel any emotions at all?\n\nPatient: Lost interest? I don't know about losing interest, exactly. I mean, I still get my work done, mostly. Just in pieces, you know? But I guess I don't have that same spark I used to. Like, I'll just leave client follow-ups sitting in my inbox for days. And I haven't been going to jazz nights downtown or playing pickup basketball lately, and I don't even really care that I'm missing it. \n\nI used to play basketball every Thursday night at this rec center over in Hyde Park. They had this beautiful old hardwood floor that they kept perfectly polished. You had to wear specific non-marking shoes or the guy who ran the desk, Mr. Henderson, would yell at you. He was this grumpy older guy, always wearing these vintage tracksuits. Like, full velour tracksuits in the middle of summer. I think he got them from this thrift store over in Northside. Northside is great for thrift shopping, actually. I found this amazing mid-century modern lamp there once, real brass, heavy as hell. I had to lug it three blocks to my car in the pouring rain. My umbrella completely inverted on me because the wind was so bad. Cincinnati weather is just completely unpredictable. One minute it's sunny, the next it's a torrential downpour. It makes dressing for the office a total nightmare, honestly. You have to layer, but then the heating in my building is always cranked way too high, so I'm sweating in my suit...\n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my emotions. Yeah, I don't know. When my friends text me to ask what's going on or why I haven't been around, I just give them these really short answers. It just feels like talking or explaining things takes way more effort than it should right now. I just can't be bothered.\n\nDoctor: When you see these signals, do you feel entirely certain that these people are intentionally communicating with you, or is there a part of you that wonders if you might be misinterpreting these events?\n\nPatient: Am I entirely certain? Well, like I was saying before, I do try to tell myself that sometimes. I try to say it could all just be a coincidence. But then I look at the actual details, like the physical invitations they send me. \n\nThey're always printed on this very specific, heavy cardstock with those rough, deckled edges. There's this little boutique letterpress shop down in Over-the-Rhine that makes custom stationery exactly like that. Have you been down to OTR lately? The gentrification there is just wild. I remember when I first started out as an advisor, property values down there were rock bottom, and now you can't even touch a decent loft for under half a million. My buddy Greg bought a place there five years ago and made an absolute killing on it. Greg actually just got a golden retriever puppy, by the way. Cutest thing ever, named him Barnaby. But man, puppies are so much work. He was telling me the dog chewed through his laptop charger right before a huge Zoom presentation. Can you imagine the panic? I'd completely lose my mind. I always keep backup chargers in my briefcase now, just in case. You never know when your battery is gonna die, especially with how much these new financial modeling apps drain the CPU on my laptop. I had to upgrade my RAM twice last year just to keep up...\n\nBut, um, what was the question? Oh, if I'm misinterpreting things. Yeah, I mean, even when I sit there and try to convince myself I'm misinterpreting who they seat me next to or what the panel topics are, I just can't really let it go. I'm still convinced those arts and finance people are tracking my clients. The signals are just too specific to ignore.\n\nDoctor: When you are speaking, do you feel that your thoughts are often pulled away from your original point by these other details that come to mind?\n\nPatient: Pulled away? I mean, I guess so. It's just that there's always so much connected to everything, you know? Like, you ask me about my thoughts, and immediately I think about how the human brain processes information. I read this article recently in some science magazine—I think it was National Geographic, actually. My dentist always has those in his waiting room. Dr. Evans. He's been my dentist since I was like, ten. He has this massive saltwater aquarium in the lobby with these incredible clownfish. They just dart in and out of the anemones all day. It’s actually really relaxing to watch before you have to go sit in the chair and get your teeth scraped. I hate the sound of that metal tool they use. It’s like nails on a chalkboard. Speaking of chalkboards, did you know they still use them in some of the older lecture halls at the university? I had an econ professor who refused to use whiteboards. He said the dust helped him think. He was a brilliant guy, though, made a fortune trading commodities back in the eighties before the regulations got so tight...\n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, right. My thoughts getting pulled away. Yeah, I don't really know if they get pulled away, exactly. I just have a lot going on up there, I guess. I haven't really thought of it as a problem.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Prefer to be by myself? I mean, I guess it might look like that. Like I mentioned a minute ago, when my friends reach out to see what's going on, I just shoot back these really short answers. It’s not that I necessarily want to be totally alone, it’s just that talking and explaining myself feels like way more effort than it should. Even just texting back feels exhausting. 

I actually need to get a new phone anyway, the screen on mine is completely shattered. I dropped it getting out of my car last week. I drive this older Honda Civic, and the seats sit really low to the ground, so you kind of have to haul yourself out of it. It gets great gas mileage, though, which is a lifesaver right now. Have you seen gas prices lately? It's absolutely ridiculous. I try to fill up at this Kroger near my apartment because I get the fuel points, but the line is always backed up into the street. People get so aggressive waiting for the pumps, too. I saw this guy in a pickup truck completely lay on his horn just because the woman in front of him took an extra five seconds to put her gas cap back on. People are just so on edge these days. I think it's the post-pandemic stress, everyone forgot how to act in public...

But, um, sorry, what did you ask me? Oh, about stopping trying with relationships. Yeah, I don't know. I just don't have that same spark I used to have for any of it. I still manage to get my work done, mostly in pieces, but I'll leave client follow-ups sitting there for days. And like I said, I skip the jazz nights and the pickup basketball games, and I just don't have much feeling about it either way. It’s just easier to not engage.

Northside is great for thrift shopping, actually. I found this amazing mid-century modern lamp there once, real brass, heavy as hell. I had to lug it three blocks to my car in the pouring rain. My umbrella completely inverted on me because the wind was so bad. Cincinnati weather is just completely unpredictable. One minute it's sunny, the next it's a torrential downpour. It makes dressing for the office a total nightmare, honestly. You have to layer, but then the heating in my building is always cranked way too high, so I'm sweating in my suit...\n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my emotions. Yeah, I don't know. When my friends text me to ask what's going on or why I haven't been around, I just give them these really short answers. It just feels like talking or explaining things takes way more effort than it should right now. I just can't be bothered.\n\nDoctor: When you see these signals, do you feel entirely certain that these people are intentionally communicating with you, or is there a part of you that wonders if you might be misinterpreting these events?\n\nPatient: Am I entirely certain? Well, like I was saying before, I do try to tell myself that sometimes. I try to say it could all just be a coincidence. But then I look at the actual details, like the physical invitations they send me. \n\nThey're always printed on this very specific, heavy cardstock with those rough, deckled edges. I always keep backup chargers in my briefcase now, just in case. You never know when your battery is gonna die, especially with how much these new financial modeling apps drain the CPU on my laptop. I had to upgrade my RAM twice last year just to keep up...\n\nBut, um, what was the question? Oh, if I'm misinterpreting things. Yeah, I mean, even when I sit there and try to convince myself I'm misinterpreting who they seat me next to or what the panel topics are, I just can't really let it go. I'm still convinced those arts and finance people are tracking my clients. My thoughts getting pulled away. Yeah, I don't really know if they get pulled away, exactly. I just have a lot going on up there, I guess.
